This means builders can scale agent workflows globally while strictly controlling access to private backends and easily customizing their agentâs tools and runtimes.&quot;],&quot;imgPreview&quot;:[0,&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/3GBib7OfVmhYlZN4Q7BzIu/f5fe02f2eaaecb1c5da9870bfe2c756d/Announcing_Claude_Managed_Agents_on_Cloudflare-OG.png&quot;]}],&quot;publicly_index&quot;:[0,true]}],[0,{&quot;id&quot;:[0,&quot;65sE8zefBt1cYbclYLhqjA&quot;],&quot;title&quot;:[0,&quot;Our billing pipeline was suddenly slow. The culprit was a hidden bottleneck in ClickHouse&quot;],&quot;slug&quot;:[0,&quot;clickhouse-query-plan-contention&quot;],&quot;excerpt&quot;:[0,&quot;When a partitioning change to our petabyte-scale ClickHouse cluster caused critical billing jobs to stall, standard metrics showed no obvious errors. This post explores how we identified severe lock contention in ClickHouse&#39;s query planner and built upstream patches to fix it.&quot;],&quot;featured&quot;:[0,false],&quot;html&quot;:[0,&quot;&lt;p&gt;At Cloudflare, we are heavy users of ClickHouse, an open source online analytical processing (OLAP) database. Every day, we make millions of calls to ClickHouse to determine how much users should be billed for their usage of Cloudflare products. If we don&amp;#39;t finish those jobs in a timely fashion, the invoices become very difficult to reconcile.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;This pipeline powers hundreds of millions of dollars in usage revenue, fraud systems, and more, so being delayed has major downstream implications.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Which is why it was a big problem when the daily aggregation jobs in ClickHouse â responsible for ensuring Cloudflareâs bills go out â had slowed way down, following a migration. All the usual suspects looked clean: I/O, memory, rows scanned, parts read. Everything we would normally check when a ClickHouse query is slow appeared to be normal.Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;This is the story of how we discovered a hidden bottleneck buried deep within ClickHouseâs internals, and the three patches we wrote to fix it.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;the-setup-a-petabyte-scale-analytics-platform\&quot;&gt;The setup: a petabyte-scale analytics platform&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#the-setup-a-petabyte-scale-analytics-platform\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;We use ClickHouse to store over a hundred petabytes of data across a few dozen clusters. To simplify onboarding for our many internal teams, we built a system called &amp;quot;Ready-Analytics&amp;quot; in early 2022.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The premise is simple: instead of designing new tables, teams can stream data into a single, massive table. Datasets are disambiguated by a &lt;code&gt;namespace&lt;/code&gt;, and each record uses a standard schema (e.g., 20 float fields, 20 string fields, a timestamp, and an &lt;code&gt;indexID&lt;/code&gt;).Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;In ClickHouse, the way data is sorted is crucial to query performance. This is where the &lt;code&gt;indexID&lt;/code&gt; comes into play. Itâs a string field, which forms part of the primary key, meaning that every individual namespace can have its data sorted in a way that is optimal for the queries the owners of that namespace expect to be running. Altogether, we end up with a primary key that looks like this: (&lt;code&gt;namespace&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;indexID&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;timestamp&lt;/code&gt;).&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;This system is popular, with hundreds of applications using it. It had already grown to more than 2PiB of data by December 2024, and an ingestion rate of millions of rows per second. But it had one critical flaw: its retention policy.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;the-problem-one-retention-policy-to-rule-them-all\&quot;&gt;The problem: one retention policy to rule them all&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#the-problem-one-retention-policy-to-rule-them-all\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Cloudflare has been using ClickHouse for many years, since before it had native Time-to-Live (TTL) features. Consequently, we built our own retention system based on partitioning. The Ready-Analytics table was partitioned by &lt;code&gt;day&lt;/code&gt;, and our retention job simply dropped partitions older than 31 days.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;This &amp;quot;one-size-fits-all&amp;quot; 31-day retention was a major limitation. Some teams needed to store data for years due to legal or contractual obligations, while others needed only a few days. This restriction meant these use cases couldn&amp;#39;t use Ready-Analytics and had to opt for a conventional setup, which has a far more complex onboarding process.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;We needed a new system that allowed &lt;b&gt;per-namespace retention&lt;/b&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;the-solution-a-new-partitioning-scheme\&quot;&gt;The solution: a new partitioning scheme&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#the-solution-a-new-partitioning-scheme\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;We considered two main approaches:&lt;/p&gt;&lt;ol&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;A Table-per-Namespace:&lt;/b&gt; This would naturally solve the retention problem but would require significant new automation to manage thousands of tables on demand.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;A New Partitioning Key:&lt;/b&gt; We could change the partitioning key from just &lt;code&gt;(day)&lt;/code&gt; to &lt;code&gt;(namespace, day)&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;/ol&gt;&lt;p&gt;We chose the second option. This would allow our existing retention system to continue managing partitions, but now with per-namespace granularity.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;We knew this would increase the total number of data parts in the table, but we made a key assumption: &lt;b&gt;since every query is filtered by a specific namespace, the &lt;/b&gt;&lt;b&gt;&lt;i&gt;number of parts read by any single query&lt;/i&gt;&lt;/b&gt;&lt;b&gt; shouldn&amp;#39;t change.&lt;/b&gt; We believed this meant performance would be unaffected.&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/6d3ywoUP3OE2DCRAJtAgF7/5aa62c5702a923e30889ebf787925ae8/BLOG-3299_image4.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;BLOG-3299 image4\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1999\&quot; height=\&quot;1983\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;sup&gt;&lt;i&gt;This shows how we changed the partitioning, allowing us to cheaply drop data for a single namespace&lt;/i&gt;&lt;/sup&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;This new system also allowed us to build a sophisticated storage management layer. Using the&lt;a href=\&quot;https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Max-min_fairness\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt; &lt;u&gt;max-min fairness algorithm&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, we could set a target disk utilization (e.g., 90%) and automatically &amp;quot;share&amp;quot; available space. Namespaces using less than their fair share would cede their unused capacity to those that needed more. This allowed us to confidently run our clusters at 90% utilization.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;We began the migration in January 2025. Using ClickHouse&amp;#39;s &lt;code&gt;Merge&lt;/code&gt; table feature, we combined the old and new tables, writing all new data to the new partitioned table while the old data aged out.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;the-mystery-when-billing-starts-to-break\&quot;&gt;The mystery: when billing starts to break&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#the-mystery-when-billing-starts-to-break\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Two months later, in late March 2025, our billing team reported that their daily aggregation jobs were slowing down. These jobs are time-critical; if they don&amp;#39;t finish, bills don&amp;#39;t go out. The jobs were getting progressively slower, and we were approaching a deadline.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;We investigated, but none of the usual suspects were to blame. I/O was fine. Memory was fine. The metrics for individual queries showed they were &lt;i&gt;not&lt;/i&gt; reading more data or more parts than before. Our initial assumption seemed correct, yet the system was grinding to a halt.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;It took several days before we even had a theory. Finally, we made a plot of query duration against the &lt;i&gt;total part count&lt;/i&gt; in the cluster. The correlation was undeniable.&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/69SY5BVn3bPW5O9n5LrVjU/c7b99fda71d57156fa2784947db675f4/BLOG-3299_image2.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;BLOG-3299 image2\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1999\&quot; height=\&quot;656\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;sup&gt;&lt;i&gt;Average SELECT Query Durations on the Ready Analytics ClickHouse Cluster, showing progressive performance degradation.&lt;/i&gt;&lt;/sup&gt;&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/2mt08KB04Y3n0vsNJAfK81/86da06fe572126e0e777f7dc4c00d7fb/BLOG-3299_image1.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;BLOG-3299 image1\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1999\&quot; height=\&quot;651\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;sup&gt;&lt;i&gt;Linear Growth in Total Data Part Count per Table Replica, following the new (namespace, day) partitioning scheme.&lt;/i&gt;&lt;/sup&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;But &lt;i&gt;why&lt;/i&gt;? If we weren&amp;#39;t &lt;i&gt;reading&lt;/i&gt; the extra parts, why did their mere existence slow us down?&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;the-investigation-hunting-bottlenecks-with-flame-graphs\&quot;&gt;The investigation: hunting bottlenecks with flame graphs&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#the-investigation-hunting-bottlenecks-with-flame-graphs\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;We turned to ClickHouse&amp;#39;s built-in &lt;a href=\&quot;https://clickhouse.com/docs/operations/system-tables/trace_log\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;code&gt;&lt;u&gt;trace_log&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/a&gt; to generate flame graphs. This is a built-in table that records traces from the running ClickHouse server. It not only includes traces of what code is being executed, but it associates these with specific users, query IDs and other metadata, meaning you can filter down to quite precise sets of events if necessary. In our case, we wanted to look specifically at &lt;i&gt;leaf SELECT queries&lt;/i&gt;. This was easy thanks to the available metadata in this table.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The first CPU-based flame graph quickly confirmed our suspicion: a huge amount of time was being spent in &lt;b&gt;query planning&lt;/b&gt;. This is the phase &lt;i&gt;before&lt;/i&gt; execution when ClickHouse decides which parts to read.&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/4Tppo6pF4ePCWTTC1lWi03/a1582ce51ef60116a1848cab83fe9516/BLOG-3299_image7.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;BLOG-3299 image7\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1999\&quot; height=\&quot;1030\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;sup&gt;&lt;i&gt;Flame graph showing that 45% of leaf query CPU time is spent filtering a vector of parts based on the partition ID&lt;/i&gt;&lt;/sup&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The flame graph was clear: 45% of the sampled CPU time was being spent in a single function called &lt;code&gt;filterPartsByPartition&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Our first attempt at a fix was a small patch to this exact code path. The planner evaluates heuristics to prune parts, and we believed they weren&amp;#39;t being evaluated in the optimal order for our table. Our patch changed the order, yielding a small 5% improvement. We were on the right path, but we&amp;#39;d missed the real problem.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;We had been generating &amp;quot;CPU&amp;quot; traces, which only sample active threads. We switched to &amp;quot;Real&amp;quot; traces, which sample &lt;i&gt;all&lt;/i&gt; threads, including those that are inactive or waiting. The new flame graph was a revelation.&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/715i8uMnak2T9Sdf4Jtcsy/1c962dbef3692257679136f6c3d36dca/BLOG-3299_image10.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;BLOG-3299 image10\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1999\&quot; height=\&quot;981\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;sup&gt;&lt;i&gt;Flame graph showing that more than half of leaf query duration is spent waiting for a mutex that protects the list of active parts&lt;/i&gt;&lt;/sup&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The problem wasn&amp;#39;t CPU-bound work; it was &lt;b&gt;massive lock contention&lt;/b&gt;. More than half of our query duration was spent &lt;i&gt;waiting&lt;/i&gt; to acquire a single mutex (&lt;code&gt;MergeTreeData&lt;/code&gt;) that protects the table&amp;#39;s list of parts. To plan a query, every single thread had to:&lt;/p&gt;&lt;ol&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Acquire an &lt;b&gt;exclusive lock&lt;/b&gt; on this mutex.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Make a complete copy of the list of &lt;i&gt;all&lt;/i&gt; parts in the table.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Release the lock.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Filter that list down to the relevant parts.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;/ol&gt;&lt;p&gt;With tens of thousands of parts and hundreds of concurrent queries, they were all just standing in a single-file line.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;the-fixes-a-trio-of-patches\&quot;&gt;The fixes: a trio of patches&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#the-fixes-a-trio-of-patches\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;This insight helped us plan a series of optimizations to alleviate these hotspots. As with all the patches we make to ClickHouse, we try to make them generic, and eventually get them contributed to the upstream codebase. This makes it easier for us to maintain our fork, and means the community benefits from the changes we make too!&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;optimization-1-use-a-shared-lock\&quot;&gt;Optimization 1: use a shared lock&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#optimization-1-use-a-shared-lock\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;The query planner doesn&amp;#39;t &lt;i&gt;modify&lt;/i&gt; the parts list; it just &lt;i&gt;reads&lt;/i&gt; it. It had no business using an exclusive lock.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;The Fix:&lt;/b&gt; We modified the code to acquire a &lt;b&gt;shared lock&lt;/b&gt; (&lt;code&gt;std::shared_lock&lt;/code&gt;) instead. This allowed all query planners to enter the critical section concurrently.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;The Result:&lt;/b&gt; A massive, immediate drop in query duration. The lock contention vanished.&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/5Kj9AqPYFnhQjQya9C1U5y/122aff70ba4aae88c7fdbc479edd0147/BLOG-3299_image8.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;BLOG-3299 image8\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1999\&quot; height=\&quot;656\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;sup&gt;&lt;i&gt;Immediate Impact of the Shared Lock Optimization (Optimization 1) on Average SELECT Query Durations, demonstrating the resolution of lock contention.&lt;/i&gt;&lt;/sup&gt;&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;optimization-2-stop-copying-the-vector\&quot;&gt;Optimization 2: stop copying the vector&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#optimization-2-stop-copying-the-vector\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Performance was significantly better, but still not back to baseline. We went back to the trace log and made another âRealâ flame graph.&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/77AAAaeIqq6wRHaG8LjVeI/6dccdbf2c8b0c334c1d05b930572ab05/BLOG-3299_image5.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;BLOG-3299 image5\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1999\&quot; height=\&quot;980\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;sup&gt;&lt;i&gt;Flame graph showing that we spend a quarter of leaf query duration copying the vector of all parts, and another quarter filtering through it (copying again).&lt;/i&gt;&lt;/sup&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The new flame graph showed the bottleneck had simply moved. Now, time was being spent &lt;i&gt;copying&lt;/i&gt; the giant vector of parts, even with the shared lock. Intuitively, copying a vector sounds cheap, but when it contains tens of thousands of elements, and you do it hundreds of times a second, it adds up.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;The Fix:&lt;/b&gt; We deferred the copy entirely. We created a &amp;quot;shared copy&amp;quot; of the parts list. Read-only operations (like query planning) just read from this copy. Any operation that &lt;i&gt;modifies&lt;/i&gt; the set of parts (like a new insert) regenerates the cache. Planners now only copy the &lt;i&gt;filtered&lt;/i&gt; list of parts they actually need.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;The Result:&lt;/b&gt; Another significant performance improvement.&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/1d63suwP2kJZPqbzMQN1XQ/8ed1c519d1929f27d920d5a740b6b09f/BLOG-3299_image6.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;BLOG-3299 image6\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1999\&quot; height=\&quot;660\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;sup&gt;&lt;i&gt;Further Performance Improvement After Rolling Out the Vector Copy Optimization (Optimization 2).&lt;/i&gt;&lt;/sup&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;After seeing these massive savings internally, we decided to bring these changes to the community. After some small design iterations with the maintainers at ClickHouse Inc., we got the changes merged under&lt;a href=\&quot;https://github.com/ClickHouse/ClickHouse/pull/85535\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt; &lt;u&gt;PR #85535&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;&lt;i&gt;.&lt;/i&gt; They have been available since &lt;a href=\&quot;https://clickhouse.com/docs/whats-new/changelog/2025#performance-improvement-1\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;ClickHouse version 25.11&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;optimization-3-binary-search-for-parts\&quot;&gt;Optimization 3: binary search for parts&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#optimization-3-binary-search-for-parts\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;We&amp;#39;re still not done. As part counts grow, performance &lt;i&gt;still&lt;/i&gt; degrades, just much more slowly. The correlation with part count was still there. Coming back to this after a few months, a new flame graph (looking the same as Figure 3) shows the time is spent in the filtering code path (the one we tried to fix first). This code performs a &lt;b&gt;linear scan&lt;/b&gt; over all parts, evaluating predicates against each one. Over a few months, we were back to select durations from before the optimizations.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;But we know this list of parts is sorted by the partitioning key. Remember that the first column of the partition key is namespace, which the vast majority of queries filter on, because it identifies the âtenant.â How can we make use of this?&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;The Fix:&lt;/b&gt; We implemented a binary search based on the &lt;code&gt;namespace&lt;/code&gt; part of the partition ID. This works because the vector is sorted, so you can filter out a lot of the entries without actually looking at them. This is particularly effective since the &lt;code&gt;namespace&lt;/code&gt; is the first part of that sorting key. After this first-pass of binary search, we have a much smaller range of parts we need to examine, and for those we still step through each one, applying the same logic as before to exclude parts based on other conditions.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;The Result:&lt;/b&gt; After deploying this patch in March 2026, query durations dropped by 50% (see Figure 8). More importantly, this finally breaks correlation of query durations with the number of parts. Unfortunately, this solution doesnât generalize that well for arbitrary query conditions (e.g. conditions such as &lt;code&gt;namespace in (5,10)&lt;/code&gt;). We are looking into more generic approaches like extending the &lt;a href=\&quot;https://clickhouse.com/blog/introducing-the-clickhouse-query-condition-cache\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;query condition cache&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; to cover part filtering.&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/4leNlnUeD3RIQVPT2VN1IB/90b76933cfdd24955c51d4c00287b9b0/BLOG-3299_image3.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;BLOG-3299 image3\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1999\&quot; height=\&quot;669\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;sup&gt;&lt;i&gt;Sustained Latency Reduction Following the Implementation of Binary Search for Part Pruning (Optimization 3).&lt;/i&gt;&lt;/sup&gt;&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;an-uneasy-truce\&quot;&gt;An uneasy truce&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#an-uneasy-truce\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;These optimizations resolved the immediate crisis with the billing system. But this journey exposed the deep, non-obvious costs of our partitioning choice.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Other problems remain. In this blog post weâve only described the problems increasing part counts had on our select durations, but it has also caused problems for ZooKeeper, which tracks metadata for all the parts in ClickHouse. Perhaps one day weâll tell the story of the 100 gigabyte ZooKeeper cluster.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;We&amp;#39;ve bought ourselves significant breathing room, but the fundamental question remains: Was this partitioning scheme the right long-term choice? Or will we eventually need to bite the bullet and move to a different architecture? For now, our patches are holding, but the experience was a clear example of how even a well-planned change can fall victim to incorrect assumptions.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;When the billing team first reported this problem we had 30,000 parts per replica. The part rate never stopped growing, and a year later we hit 160k parts per replica, but query durations have been stable thanks to the optimizations we made here.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;At Cloudflare, we solve complex engineering problems at a massive scale. If the debugging and optimizations we described here sound like the type of challenge youâre looking for, check out some of the &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.cloudflare.com/careers/jobs/?department=Engineering\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;open roles&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; we are hiring Hereâs how.&quot;],&quot;featured&quot;:[0,false],&quot;html&quot;:[0,&quot;&lt;p&gt;Weâve enabled higher usage limits, faster performance, and better reliability for Browser Run by rebuilding on top of Cloudflareâs &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/containers/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Containers&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;You can now spin up 60 browsers per minute via the Workers binding and run up to 120 concurrently â 4x the previous limit. Also, &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/browser-run/quick-actions/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Quick Action&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; response times dropped more than 50%. You don&amp;#39;t need to change anything: these improvements are live today. On top of that, weâre shipping fixes and new features faster than before. Read on to learn how we did it and see the data.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;remind-me-what-is-browser-run\&quot;&gt;Remind me: what is Browser Run?&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#remind-me-what-is-browser-run\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;&lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/browser-run/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Browser Run&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; enables developers to programmatically control and interact with headless browser instances running on Cloudflareâs global network. Thatâs useful for end-to-end testing of web applications, securely investigating suspicious URLs, and leveraging how browsers can easily render PDF documents, amongst other quick actions like capturing screenshots and extracting content. More recently, itâs become a critical enabler of AI agents to interact with the web. Weâre building Browser Run to be the go-to platform to responsibly utilize automated browsers securely at massive scale.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;outgrowing-our-bunk-bed\&quot;&gt;Outgrowing our bunk bed&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#outgrowing-our-bunk-bed\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Before adopting Cloudflare Containers, we shared infrastructure with &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.cloudflare.com/learning/access-management/what-is-browser-isolation/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Browser Isolation&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; (BISO). While technically similar, BISOâs larger container images slowed startup and development. Crucially, BISO browsers lacked optimal global distribution, compromising resiliency and latency. Additionally, typical BISO usersâ long, steady sessions clashed with Browser Runâs short, spiky usage, creating scaling bottlenecks and availability delays.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Thankfully, after much internal development, Cloudflare released &lt;a href=\&quot;https://blog.cloudflare.com/cloudflare-containers-coming-2025/\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Durable Object (DO)-enabled ContainersÂ  open beta last year&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, meaning we were ready for a tentative adoption that ultimately benefited both product platforms. Like most successful product platforms, weâre committed to building on our own platform wherever feasible so that we can feel and fix any pain points ahead of any external customers.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;the-migration-containers\&quot;&gt;The migration: Containers&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#the-migration-containers\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;We started a gradual migration by inserting a Worker in our incoming request paths to provide some Container-powered browsers to a handful of users alongside those from BISO. This dual support during development was key: it allowed us to compare performance, isolate implementation bugs and ultimately gain confidence in the benefits of the Container-driven approach.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Ramping up adoption, we first used the Container browsers for all of our Quick Actions endpoints, then for connections via the Workers browser binding on free accounts, followed by pay-as-you-go accounts in order to validate stability before we rolled it out to all remaining contract customers, ensuring a transition that required no action or existing worker redeployments from our customers.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;challenges-performance-and-scale-bottlenecks\&quot;&gt;Challenges: performance and scale bottlenecks&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#challenges-performance-and-scale-bottlenecks\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;On our end, though, we faced a fresh set of challenges getting familiar with a novel, unstable early-stage Containers platform interface that was light on documentation, light on observability, and light on colleagues in an overlapping timezone. However, our feedback to our own teams as &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.cloudflare.com/en-gb/the-net/top-of-mind-security/customer-zero/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Customer Zero&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; meant that we could provide a tight feedback loop leading to substantial upgrades that benefit our external customers too. Nevertheless, there was a lot of friction to overcome initially, most of which were to be expected for a closed beta in active development. Other hurdles to overcome were intrinsic to the new technical environment.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;For example, once our browsers could run globally, our architecture had to adapt. DO-enabled Containers create a Durable Object as close to the incoming request as possible, but the connected Container may spin up on the other side of the world. This works fine for one-shot messages like &amp;quot;start my app,&amp;quot; but when you&amp;#39;re establishing a WebSocket between them and exchanging dozens of messages for a screenshot request, those extra milliseconds crossing the globe start adding up.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Our solution? Create regional pools of pre-warmed DO-backed browser containers to constrain the max distance (and hence max latency) between DOs and containers. When a request comes in, we pick a DO-container pair closest to the user within that region. This keeps latency low on both hops: user to DO, and DO to container. It adds a few more moving parts to our overall architecture, but we figured that was worthwhile so long as we had observability into the global state of each browser so that we could allocate and re-allocate capacity according to changing demand. A perfect use case for &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/kv/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Workers KV&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;â¦to a point.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Demand for our headless browsers has been ramping up since the beginning of last year. In short, AI agent builders discovered Browser Run and quickly brought request volumes outpacing our existing capacity. We quickly hit the limits of how quickly we could adjust our pool capacity to serve this new demand with a scalable approach. KVâs &lt;i&gt;eventual&lt;/i&gt; consistency of around 30 seconds was becoming a bottleneck on our critical request path. You might check KV, see a container as &amp;quot;available,&amp;quot; but by the time you route to it (30 seconds later), it&amp;#39;s already claimed. That lag creates race conditions and overallocation of browsers, severely limiting how fast we could scale to meet demand spikes.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;migrating-from-kv-to-d1-queues\&quot;&gt;Migrating from KV to D1 + Queues&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#migrating-from-kv-to-d1-queues\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;We previously stored each container state in KV. This meant that we could keep getting a minute old state due to cache TTL (recently &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/changelog/post/2026-01-30-kv-reduced-minimum-cachettl/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;KV changed the minimum cache TTL to 30 seconds&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;, but even so that value is still too high).&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;We decided to migrate the container state into &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/d1/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;D1&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; instances instead. D1&amp;#39;s transactional nature is a good fit here. Once we assign a browser to a user, it&amp;#39;s exclusively theirs. Browsers are not shared resources. SQLite transactions ensure atomic assignment and prevent race conditions where two requests might claim the same browser simultaneously.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Hereâs a simplified version of our browser acquisition query:&lt;/p&gt;\n            &lt;pre class=\&quot;language-SQL\&quot;&gt;&lt;code class=\&quot;language-SQL\&quot;&gt;WITH candidate_pool AS (\n    -- candidate pool logic to pick based on latency and other rules\n)\nUPDATE containers\nSET status = &amp;#039;picked&amp;#039;\nWHERE sessionId IN (\n    SELECT sessionId\n    FROM candidate_pool\n    ORDER BY RANDOM()\n    LIMIT ?5\n)\nRETURNING data&lt;/pre&gt;&lt;/code&gt;\n            &lt;p&gt;We keep D1 shards &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/d1/configuration/data-location/#available-location-hints\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;per location&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; and given that we may have several thousand containers running, and that each container needs to update its state every 5 seconds, we kept running into a problem: we would &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/d1/platform/limits/#concurrency-and-throughput\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;overload the database&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. For instance, if each write takes 1ms we can only write at most 1,000 times, which at one row per write would mean that we could only have 5,000 containers before overloading the database.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;However, if we batch those writes, we can get much higher values, because batch writes are not significantly longer than individual ones, so we can increase the throughput in orders of magnitude. In our case, we use 100 row batches, which means we can now update a maximum of 500,000 containers per location. This headroom means capacity planning is no longer a bottleneck.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Currently, our P95 for batch write is 0.1ms!&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;To batch writes, we use &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.cloudflare.com/developer-platform/products/cloudflare-queues/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Queues&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;: every 5 seconds, each container computes its own state and adds it to its location queue. We then &lt;a href=\&quot;https://developers.cloudflare.com/queues/configuration/batching-retries/#batching\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;configure a worker consumer&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; with 100 batch size and 1 second batch timeout:&lt;/p&gt;\n            &lt;pre class=\&quot;language-JSON\&quot;&gt;&lt;code class=\&quot;language-JSON\&quot;&gt;{\n    ...\n    &amp;quot;queues&amp;quot;: {\n        &amp;quot;consumers&amp;quot;: [\n            {\n                &amp;quot;queue&amp;quot;: &amp;quot;production-core-containers-queue-weur&amp;quot;,\n                &amp;quot;max_batch_size&amp;quot;: 100,\n                &amp;quot;max_batch_timeout&amp;quot;: 1,\n                &amp;quot;max_retries&amp;quot;: 1,\n            },\n            ...\n        ]\n        ...\n    }\n}&lt;/pre&gt;&lt;/code&gt;\n            &lt;p&gt;With this configuration, we achieve acceptable lag times well below 2 seconds. That said, queue backlogs can still cause stale state. When this happens, each region falls back to a designated backup region until the primary queue catches up.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;additional-perks-for-quick-actions\&quot;&gt;Additional perks for quick actions&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#additional-perks-for-quick-actions\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;With dedicated infrastructure, we could now make upgrades to the browser container image without unwanted side effects or bloat for other products like BISO. This opened the door to optimize quick actions like screenshots and content extraction. Previously, our workers established a WebSocket to the remote browser and sent instructions one at a time: open a page, navigate to the URL, wait for it to load and take the screenshot. Each step had to be completed before the next could begin.Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;However, now we send all parameters in a single HTTP request directly to the container, and the entire flow executes internally without any back-and-forth between the worker and browser.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;results-massive-performance-boost-and-increased-limits\&quot;&gt;Results: massive performance boost and increased limits&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#results-massive-performance-boost-and-increased-limits\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;DevTools Protocol&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; messages.&lt;/p&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/3eIJK055QLIl3ay12TIhfQ/9d988169efadb16cb5b18b539b62aa55/image3.png\&quot; However, now that we run our own container image, we can upgrade at a faster tempo. For example, WebGL, a much-requested feature, is now available for browser-based rendering along with WebMCP (Model Context Protocol for the web) which enables new agentic interaction patterns. Both are made possible because we can control the browser version and flags without unwanted side effects in other Cloudflare products.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;In a nutshell, weâre just getting started with unleashing the power of browsers at scale, especially for agentic development. Cloudflareâs Security and Engineering teams began assessing the vulnerability as soon as it was disclosed. We reviewed the exploit technique, evaluated exposure across our infrastructure, and validated that our existing behavioral detections could identify the exploit pattern within minutes.Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;There was no impact to the Cloudflare environment, no customer data was at risk, and no services were disrupted at any point.&lt;/b&gt; Read on to learn how our preparedness paid off.Â &lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;background\&quot;&gt;Background&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#background\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;Cloudflare operates a global Linux server infrastructure at an immense scale, with datacenters located &lt;a href=\&quot;https://www.cloudflare.com/network/\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;across 330 cities&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;. We maintain a custom Linux kernel build based on the community&amp;#39;s Long-Term Support (LTS) versions to manage updates effectively at this volume. At any given time, we may utilize multiple LTS versions from various series, such as 6.12 or 6.18, which benefit from extended update periods.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The community regularly merges and releases security and stability updates which trigger an automated job to generate a new internal kernel build approximately every week. These builds undergo testing in our staging data centers to ensure stability before a global rollout. Following a successful release, the Edge Reboot Release (ERR) pipeline manages a systematic update and reboot of the edge infrastructure on a four-week cycle. Our control plane infrastructure typically adopts the most recent kernel, with reboots scheduled according to specific workload requirements.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;By the time a CVE becomes public knowledge, the necessary fix has typically been integrated into stable Linux LTS releases for several weeks. Our established procedures ensure that we have already deployed these patches.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;At the time of the &amp;quot;Copy Fail&amp;quot; disclosure, the majority of our infrastructure was running the 6.12 LTS version, while a subset of machines had begun transitioning to the newer 6.18 LTS release.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;about-the-copy-fail-vulnerability\&quot;&gt;About the Copy Fail vulnerability&lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#about-the-copy-fail-vulnerability\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;It helps to understand the vulnerability before getting to the response story. A comprehensive write-up can be found in the original &lt;a href=\&quot;https://xint.io/blog/copy-fail-linux-distributions\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;Xint Code disclosure&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt; post.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h4 id=\&quot;af_alg-and-the-kernel-crypto-api\&quot;&gt;AF_ALG and the kernel crypto API&lt;/h4&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#af_alg-and-the-kernel-crypto-api\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;The Linux kernel&amp;#39;s internal crypto API manages functions like kTLS and IPsec. Userspace programs access this via the &lt;code&gt;AF_ALG&lt;/code&gt; socket family, allowing unprivileged processes to request encryption or decryption. The &lt;code&gt;algif_aead&lt;/code&gt; module facilitates this for Authenticated Encryption with Associated Data (AEAD) ciphers.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;An unprivileged program follows these steps:&lt;/p&gt;&lt;ol&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Opens an &lt;code&gt;AF_ALG&lt;/code&gt; socket and binds to an AEAD template.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Sets a key and accepts a request socket.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Submits input via &lt;code&gt;sendmsg()&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;splice()&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Executes the operation using &lt;code&gt;recvmsg()&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;/ol&gt;&lt;p&gt;The &lt;code&gt;splice()&lt;/code&gt; system call is critical here, as it moves data by passing page cache references.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h4 id=\&quot;memory-mechanics-page-cache-and-in-place-crypto\&quot;&gt;Memory mechanics: page cache and in-place crypto&lt;/h4&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#memory-mechanics-page-cache-and-in-place-crypto\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;The &lt;b&gt;page cache&lt;/b&gt; is a shared system cache for file contents. Modifying a page belonging to a setuid binary effectively edits that program for all users until the page is evicted.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The crypto API utilizes &lt;b&gt;scatterlists&lt;/b&gt;, which are structures linking various memory pages. In 2017, &lt;code&gt;algif_aead&lt;/code&gt; was optimized for &lt;i&gt;in-place&lt;/i&gt; operations, chaining destination and reference pages together. This design lacked enforcement to prevent algorithms from writing past intended boundaries.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h4 id=\&quot;the-vulnerability-out-of-bounds-write\&quot;&gt;The vulnerability: out-of-bounds write&lt;/h4&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#the-vulnerability-out-of-bounds-write\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;When the user executes &lt;code&gt;recvmsg()&lt;/code&gt;, the &lt;code&gt;authencesn&lt;/code&gt; wrapper in the kernel performs a 4-byte write past the legitimate output region:&lt;/p&gt;\n            &lt;pre class=\&quot;language-c++\&quot;&gt;&lt;code class=\&quot;language-c++\&quot;&gt;scatterwalk_map_and_copy(tmp + 1, dst, assoclen + cryptlen, 4, 1);\n&lt;/pre&gt;&lt;/code&gt;\n            &lt;p&gt;By using &lt;code&gt;splice()&lt;/code&gt;, an attacker can chain a target file&amp;#39;s page cache pages to the scatterlist. The out-of-bounds write then taints the cached file, allowing an attacker to control which file is modified, the offset, and the specific 4 bytes written. This means the attacker can manipulate the following with this exploit:&lt;/p&gt;&lt;ul&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;File: Any readable file.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Offset: Tunable via &lt;code&gt;assoclen&lt;/code&gt; and splice parameters.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;Value: Controlled via AAD bytes 4-7 in &lt;code&gt;sendmsg()&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;/ul&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h4 id=\&quot;the-exploit-step-by-step\&quot;&gt;The exploit, step by step&lt;/h4&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#the-exploit-step-by-step\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    \n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/5uqfOddH7biQaTjtgQOist/5c16c08bf3e5ce2f9030d6f98d2403cb/BLOG-3308_2.png\&quot; alt=\&quot;BLOG-3308 2\&quot; class=\&quot;kg-image\&quot; width=\&quot;1106\&quot; height=\&quot;900\&quot; loading=\&quot;lazy\&quot;/&gt;\n          &lt;/figure&gt;&lt;p&gt;The default exploit targets &lt;code&gt;/usr/bin/su&lt;/code&gt;, a setuid-root binary present on essentially every distribution.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;ol&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Cache Reference:&lt;/b&gt; Open &lt;code&gt;/usr/bin/su&lt;/code&gt; as &lt;code&gt;O_RDONLY&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;read()&lt;/code&gt; to populate the page cache. Use splice() on the file descriptor to pass these page cache references into the crypto scatterlist.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Setup:&lt;/b&gt; Create an &lt;code&gt;AF_ALG&lt;/code&gt; socket, &lt;code&gt;bind()&lt;/code&gt; to &lt;code&gt;authencesn(hmac(sha256),cbc(aes))&lt;/code&gt;, set a key, and accept a request socket without needing privileges.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Write Construction:&lt;/b&gt; For each 4-byte shellcode chunk:&lt;/p&gt;&lt;ul&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;sendmsg()&lt;/code&gt; with AAD bytes 4â7 containing the shellcode.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;splice()&lt;/code&gt; the binary into a pipe then the &lt;code&gt;AF_ALG&lt;/code&gt; socket so &lt;code&gt;assoclen + cryptlen&lt;/code&gt; targets the desired &lt;code&gt;.text&lt;/code&gt; offset.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;/ul&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Trigger:&lt;/b&gt; &lt;code&gt;recvmsg()&lt;/code&gt; initiates decryption. &lt;code&gt;authencesn&lt;/code&gt; writes its scratch data to the target offset of &lt;code&gt;/usr/bin/su&lt;/code&gt; in the page cache. Although the function returns &lt;code&gt;-EBADMSG&lt;/code&gt;, the 4-byte write is now in the global page cache.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Execution:&lt;/b&gt; Running &lt;code&gt;execve(&amp;quot;/usr/bin/su&amp;quot;)&lt;/code&gt; loads the tainted page cache. Since the binary is setuid-root, the injected shellcode executes with &lt;b&gt;root&lt;/b&gt; privileges.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;/ol&gt;&lt;p&gt;The upstream fix (&lt;a href=\&quot;https://github.com/torvalds/linux/commit/a664bf3d603d\&quot; target=\&quot;_blank\&quot; rel=\&quot;noopener noreferrer\&quot;&gt;&lt;u&gt;commit a664bf3d603d&lt;/u&gt;&lt;/a&gt;) reverts the 2017 in-place optimization, removing the exploit.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h3 id=\&quot;how-we-responded\&quot;&gt;How we respondedÂ &lt;/h3&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#how-we-responded\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;When the vulnerability was disclosed, many workstreams started in parallel:&lt;/p&gt;&lt;ul&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Mapping the blast radius:&lt;/b&gt; Our security team worked with kernel engineers to determine which kernel versions were vulnerable and assess the potential exposure.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Validating coverage:&lt;/b&gt; Security reviewed the exploit technique and confirmed that our existing behavioral detections could identify the exploit pattern during authorized internal validation.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Proactive threat hunting:&lt;/b&gt; Security began searching for signs that the vulnerability had been exploited before it was publicly known, going back 48 hours in our fleet-wide logs.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Engineering a mitigation:&lt;/b&gt; Kernel engineers began building a runtime mitigation that would protect the fleet without breaking production services.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;li&gt;&lt;p&gt;&lt;b&gt;Continuing software updates&lt;/b&gt;: Our engineering teams worked on delivering an updated Linux kernel, which required carefully rebooting and rolling it out across our servers.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;/li&gt;&lt;/ul&gt;&lt;p&gt;There was no customer impact at any point during this response.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h4 id=\&quot;validating-detection-coverage\&quot;&gt;Validating detection coverage&lt;/h4&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#validating-detection-coverage\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;One of the first things our security team did was confirm that our existing endpoint detection would catch this exploit. Our servers run behavioral detection that continuously monitors process execution patterns. It doesn&amp;#39;t rely on knowing about specific vulnerabilities; it watches for anomalous behavior across the fleet.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;When our engineers validated the vulnerability internally as part of the response, the detection platform flagged it within minutes. The system linked the entire execution chainâstarting at the script interpreter, moving through the kernelâs cryptographic subsystem, and ending at the privilege escalation binaryâflagging it as malicious based on fleet-wide behavioral patterns.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;This happened without a signature update, without a rule change, and without human intervention. Our behavioral detection coverage existed before we wrote any custom logic for this particular Copy File exploit.Â &lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The confirmation was important because it meant we had coverage before writing a vulnerability-specific rule.&lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h4 id=\&quot;hunting-for-exploitation\&quot;&gt;Hunting for exploitation&lt;/h4&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#hunting-for-exploitation\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;p&gt;While our engineering team moved to a more targeted mitigation, our security investigation had been running since disclosure. This is our standard procedure for any critical vulnerability.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;Our security team operates on a simple principle for critical vulnerabilities: assume compromise until you can prove otherwise. The investigation started from the assumption that exploitation could have occurred before the vulnerability was public, and we worked systematically to either confirm or rule it out.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;The exploit leaves a distinctive trace in kernel logs when it runs. We searched for that trace across our centralized logging infrastructure, covering 48 hours before the vulnerability was publicly disclosed. If someone had exploited this before the world knew about it, we would have seen it.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;We pulled access logs for affected systems and reconstructed who connected, when, and what commands they ran. This gave us a complete forensic picture of interactive activity on potentially affected infrastructure.&lt;/p&gt;&lt;p&gt;We checked that system binaries had not been tampered with, validated cryptographic hashes against known-good package manifests, looked for persistence mechanisms, and audited network connections for anything unusual. Everything was clean. &lt;/p&gt;\n    &lt;div class=\&quot;flex anchor relative\&quot;&gt;\n      &lt;h2 id=\&quot;incident-timeline-and-impact\&quot;&gt;Incident timeline and impact&lt;/h2&gt;\n      &lt;a href=\&quot;#incident-timeline-and-impact\&quot; aria-hidden=\&quot;true\&quot; class=\&quot;relative sm:absolute sm:-start-5\&quot;&gt;\n        &lt;svg width=\&quot;16\&quot; height=\&quot;16\&quot; viewBox=\&quot;0 0 24 24\&quot;&gt;&lt;path fill=\&quot;currentcolor\&quot; d=\&quot;m12.11 15.39-3.88 3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1-3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0-3.5l3.88-3.88a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-3.88 3.89a4.48 4.48 0 0 0 6.33 6.33l3.89-3.88a1 1 0 1 0-1.42-1.42Zm8.58-12.08a4.49 4.49 0 0 0-6.33 0l-3.89 3.88a1 1 0 0 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.88a2.52 2.52 0 0 1 3.5 0 2.47 2.47 0 0 1 0 3.5l-3.88 3.88a1 1 0 1 0 1.42 1.42l3.88-3.89a4.49 4.49 0 0 0 0-6.33ZM8.83 15.17a1 1 0 0 0 1.1.22 1 1 0 0 0 .32-.22l4.92-4.92a1 1 0 0 0-1.42-1.42l-4.92 4.92a1 1 0 0 0 0 1.42Z\&quot;&gt;&lt;/path&gt;&lt;/svg&gt;\n      &lt;/a&gt;\n    &lt;/div&gt;\n    &lt;style type=\&quot;text/css\&quot;&gt;\n.tg  {border-collapse:collapse;border-spacing:0;margin:0px auto;}\n.tg End-to-end verification on a previously-vulnerable test node confirms the exploit no longer works.&lt;/span&gt;&lt;/td&gt;\n  &lt;/tr&gt;\n  &lt;tr&gt;\n    &lt;td class=\&quot;tg-0lax\&quot;&gt;&lt;span style=\&quot;font-weight:400;font-style:normal;text-decoration:none;color:#000;background-color:transparent\&quot;&gt;2026-05-04 (morning)&lt;/span&gt;&lt;/td&gt;\n    &lt;td class=\&quot;tg-0lax\&quot;&gt;&lt;span style=\&quot;font-weight:400;font-style:normal;text-decoration:none;color:#000;background-color:transparent\&quot;&gt;Reboot automation resumed at normal pace with the patched kernel.&lt;/span&gt;&lt;/td&gt;\n  &lt;/tr&gt;\n  &lt;tr&gt;\n    &lt;td class=\&quot;tg-0lax\&quot;&gt;&lt;span style=\&quot;font-weight:400;font-style:normal;text-decoration:none;color:#000;background-color:transparent\&quot;&gt;2026-05-04 onward&lt;/span&gt;&lt;/td&gt;\n    &lt;td class=\&quot;tg-0lax\&quot;&gt;&lt;span style=\&quot;font-weight:400;font-style:normal;text-decoration:none;color:#000;background-color:transparent\&quot;&gt;Servers that had already passed through reboot automation earlier in the week manually rebooted to pick up the patched kernel. Unpatched servers update per our normal reboot automation.&lt;/span&gt;&lt;/td&gt;\n  &lt;/tr&gt;\n&lt;/tbody&gt;&lt;/table&gt;&lt;/div&gt;\n          &lt;figure class=\&quot;kg-card kg-image-card\&quot;&gt;\n          &lt;Image src=\&quot;https://cf-assets.www.cloudflare.com/zkvhlag99gkb/7LI0k0FJgbLKzOkSEtYPwW/997234a334b3694c63417fad5810b679/BLOG-3308_3.png\&quot;
